> @smokingsix Larry Fink's public statement was simply delineating two outcomes - cheap oil if supply normalizes vs VERY expensive and perhaps a global recession if not. He didn't mention anything about the US treasury controlling the oil sales directly.  
> Additionally, Iraq's oil revenues don't go to the Treasury directly. It just has strong influence and oversight since the transactions are completed in the Dollar and must be cleared through the US financial systems.  
> As far as Venezuela's oil, SOME of it that is cleared through special arrangements or sanctions relief are held in restricted accounts that the US can control. But it's not as if the entire revenue stream is just swept into the Treasury.  
> And even if you were correct, routing a couple of countries' oil proceeds alone cannot stabilize the entire currency.  
> For the time being, the Dollar is still absolutely the most dominant currency in the world. And it will not "fall" overnight.  
> It's still very important to get all the facts correct so that we don't spread false narratives. False information is more dangerous than no information.

Israel is positioned to replace American imperial dominance in the Middle East as the US military's corruption, hubris, and lack of political will cause it to lose the Iran war.

🔑 Key Points:
1. The US entered the Iran war expecting quick surrender but was caught off guard. Trump admitted America didn't expect Iran to fight back, close the Strait of Hormuz, or strike Qatar, Bahrain, and Kuwait.

2. America's war strategy forces narrative, politics, and economics to serve military goals — Iran does the reverse. Iran uses its military to strengthen its economy, split GCC alliances, and win global opinion, giving it far more strategic flexibility.

3. The US military-industrial complex is designed to steal taxpayer money, not win wars. Julian Assange is cited: the MIC's goal is never-ending wars to transfer funds to a transnational elite; America spends 41% of all global military spending yet fields ineffective systems.

4. Flagship US weapons have failed in the Iran conflict. The $13B Gerald Ford carrier retreated within 3 weeks, the $100M F-35 was shot down by Iran, and Patriot missile systems cannot stop cheap Iranian drones.

5. America faces three structural constraints that will cost it this war. Only 40% of Americans support the conflict, manufacturing capacity cannot replenish expended munitions quickly enough, and the Pentagon fears reporting casualties due to political backlash.

6. Iran gained massive economic windfalls as a direct result of the war. The US unsanctioned Iranian oil to manage Hormuz supply disruptions, delivering Iran $14B instantly — more than its entire $10B annual military budget.

7. Larry Fink of BlackRock warned that sustained Hormuz closure could push oil above $150 and trigger a global recession. He argued the war must be fought to conclusion, though the presenter notes the war itself created the Hormuz threat.

8. Israel is 'auditioning' to replace America as the Middle East empire by demonstrating unity, determination, and cost-effective operations. 82% of Israelis support Gaza expulsions, and the 2024 Lebanon pager attack — costing only $275M — demonstrated strategic precision the US cannot match.

9. The Greater Israel Project envisions territorial control from the Nile to the Euphrates, but Iran is not on the map. Israel's real strategic obstacle is removing American bases from the Middle East; Iran is being used as the mechanism to overextend and expel the US.

10. Post-war, a Pax Judaica trade network and an Iran-centered Eurasian corridor would form two competing regional orders. One aspect of this theory I struggle to envision is the U.S. descending into a civil war anytime soon. It would take major, fundamental shifts for the public to reach a point where that outcome is even desirable.

Americans still have the capacity and freedom to choose comfort and distraction—whether that’s sitting on the couch doomscrolling or watching Professor Jiang’s lectures. Bridging the gap between that reality and actively contributing to the collapse of society would require many more steps in between.

In a long enough timeline, no society is immune to that kind of breakdown but the U.S. doesn’t appear close to that tipping point.

### **@Lily-m3q8v** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0aASxQrJYuo&lc=UgwAwQWvwmckRxwRJ-N4AaABAg)

It has nothing to do with being stupid that “the weak,” as he puts it, cannot work together. That’s a stupid simplification.

The issue is that, on the one hand, because they are weak, they inevitably underestimate one another and see each other as weak. In terms of competence, they distrust each other’s abilities precisely because of that weakness. Since they view themselves—and each other—as weak, they think that if they form an alliance with someone strong, they might become a bit stronger themselves and thus be able to dominate someone weaker. So they have no real incentive to form alliances with other weak individuals in whom they have no confidence, especially when they believe they could dominate them if they were slightly stronger.

Moreover, they are weak and therefore susceptible to corruption, since “weakness” here refers to economic and expertise-related weakness. As a result, they are easily corruptible, which makes them untrustworthy—because anyone could come along, buy them off, and they would be tempted. But that doesn’t mean they are stupid. It is true that if one weak individual forms an alliance with another, there is a strong possibility that the second one could be corrupted. We’ve seen this, for example, in parts of Africa, including Mali, where people are often perceived as being corrupted against their own interests. As a result, trust becomes extremely difficult, especially in contexts of extreme poverty. So forming alliances between weak parties is inherently fragile.

Finally, a weak individual has an incentive to become stronger, which makes an alliance with a stronger party more beneficial. By contrast, an alliance with another weak party risks dragging them down further, making them even more vulnerable. That is what makes alliances between weak actors so difficult to sustain. And as long as there is the possibility of forming an alliance with someone stronger, there will always be a level of mistrust that undermines cooperation among the weak.
